An edge belongs to the shape it was selected from\n    and `chamfer` reads its target off that shape, so applying batches in sequence would\n    quietly keep re-chamfering the first body and hand it back.\n\n    This does not choose what to polish. Deciding which edges are exposed, which are\n    mating geometry and which are concave is the part's judgement and stays in the part.\n    \"\"\"\n    floor = 0.8 * size**2\n\n    def tiny(solid):\n        return len([f for f in solid.faces() if f.area < floor])\n\n    allowance = tiny(shape)\n    kept, best = [], shape\n\n    def lands(candidate):\n        try:\n            # The raw kernel call, not the guarded one: bisecting expects failures and\n            # discards them, so building the guidance text here would be advice nobody\n            # reads, once per attempt.\n            out = _chamfer(candidate, size)\n        except Exception:\n            return None\n        return out if tiny(out) <= allowance else None\n\n    def take(batch):\n        nonlocal kept, best\n        out = lands(kept + batch)\n        if out is not None:\n            kept, best = kept + batch, out\n            return\n        if len(batch) == 1:\n            return  # this one cannot land beside what is already there\n        half = len(batch) // 2\n        take(batch[:half])\n        take(batch[half:])\n\n    take(sorted(edges, key=lambda e: (-e.length, e.center().X, e.center().Y, e.center().Z)))\n    return best\n","total_lines":153}},"locations":[]}

The convexity test is here because a polish pass cannot\n# be written without it: chamfering an inside corner is the one polish mistake that looks\n# fine in code. `measured` is here because the alternative to asking for a dimension is\n# inventing one, and an invented one builds, checks clean and prints.\n# `stand` is here because a diagonal print variant cannot be written without it: the\n# rotation is trivial, but the bed facet it cuts is a doctrine rule (2mm of flat, or the\n# first layer is one extrusion line wide and peels), and a part file left to tilt by\n# hand ships the knife edge.\n# `crown` is here because rounding a variable-height rim by hand is an eight-step sweep\n40→# construction (issue #55), and every direct fillet of such a rim dies in OCCT's corner\n# capping. It is the doctrine's one sanctioned round-edge treatment.\n# `counterbore` is here because the naive alternative is two cylinders that build,\n# check as an ordinary bridge, and print a screw seat on sagging air: the stepped\n# bridging stack is fixed print-farm practice, not a judgement, so it is generated.\n# `reject` is here because a part that knows a configuration cannot work (a hole\n# narrower than the tool it holds) has to be able to say so, and the alternative is a\n# bare ValueError that the viewer can only present as a crash. A refusal through\n# `reject` keeps its message and the parameter it names, and is shown as a limit of\n# the design.\n50→# `chamfer` deliberately shadows build123d's. Same behaviour, same exception type;\n# what it adds is the doctrine's rule on the way out of a failure, because the kernel's\n# own advice is \"try a smaller length value(s)\" and following it is how a part ends up\n# with a 0.4mm chamfer that lands and prints as a defect. It is the most common way a\n# part stops building, so it is the one message worth owning. `fillet` is deliberately\n# not wrapped: build123d's fillet error already names `max_fillet()` and a smaller\n# radius really is the fix there, so the chamfer rule would be wrong half the time.\n# `import_stl` shadows build123d's for a blunter reason: build123d's returns a `Face`,\n# a sheet of triangles with no inside, and subtracting from one segfaults instead of\n# raising. A part builds inside the `nurb dev` process, so the whole watcher dies\n60→# without printing anything. It is also the first name a model tries when a downloaded\n# file lands in a project. Parts and assemblies both register through it, so they cannot\n    drift on what counts as a parameter.\n    \"\"\"\n    params = {}\n    accepts_draft = False\n    for name, p in inspect.signature(fn).parameters.items():\n        if name == \"draft\":\n            accepts_draft = True\n            continue\n        params[name] = None if p.default is inspect.Parameter.empty else p.default\n    return params, accepts_draft\n\n\ndef param_docs(fn, params):\n    \"\"\"Per-parameter descriptions, read off `name: text` lines in the docstring.\n\n    The docstring is where a function explains itself, so a slider's tooltip comes\n    from the same place a reader would look rather than from a parallel declaration.\n    Any line opening with a declared parameter's name and a colon counts, which is\n    what a Google-style Args section already writes; a deeper-indented line continues\n    the one above it, and a name the signature does not declare is ordinary prose.\n    \"\"\"\n    docs = {}\n    current, indent = None, 0\n    for line in (inspect.getdoc(fn) or \"\").splitlines():\n        m = re.match(r\"(\\s*)([A-Za-z_]\\w*)\\s*(?:\\([^)]*\\))?:\\s+(\\S.*)\", line)\n        if m and m.group(2) in params:\n            current, indent = m.group(2), len(m.group(1))\n            docs[current] = m.group(3).strip()\n        elif current and line.strip() and len(line) - len(line.lstrip()) > indent:\n            docs[current] += \" \" + line.strip()\n        else:\n            current = None\n    return docs\n\n\nclass Rejected(Exception):\n    \"\"\"A part refusing a configuration, raised by `reject`.\n\n    Its own type, subclassing nothing that gets caught by accident, so the server\n    and the CLI can tell a designed refusal from a part that actually broke.\n    \"\"\"\n\n    def __init__(self, message, param=None):\n        super().__init__(message)\n        self.param = param\n        # Filled by the builder before this crosses the server boundary. The near miss is worse\n220→    than the obvious one. A shelf that only touches its post along a tangent line reads\n    as joined in the viewer from every angle, and the kernel quietly keeps it as two.\n\n    What makes this the one rule that silences the others is that every rule after it\n    reads `shape.solids()[0]`. On a part in pieces they all describe whichever fragment\n    the kernel happened to list first, so the report fills with overhangs and floating\n    tips that are true of that fragment and beside the point. When no spec exists and calipers are not going to happen, ask for a ten-second phone scan instead: Scaniverse is free and exports STL, OBJ or GLB meshes, and `nurb scan <file>` reads one in mm, states the units it used, and `--section z` slices a cross-section into a polyline short enough to sketch against. A 3MF is the half of those downloads nurb does not read, and the fix is one menu item rather than a dead end: ask the user to open it in their slicer and export the plate as STL. A solid floor under\n    the foot lifts the bench by `lift` from measurements.toml, which is still a\n    guess until someone measures the wobble.\n\n    Pocket inner size is the measured leg plus 0.4 mm clearance, 8 mm deep, with\n    2 mm walls on all four sides. Prints pocket-up, flat on the bed.\n    \"\"\"\n    leg_width = measured(\"leg_width\")\n    leg_depth = measured(\"leg_depth\")\n    lift = measured(\"lift\")\n\n    inner_x = leg_width + _CLEARANCE\n    inner_y = leg_depth + _CLEARANCE\n    outer_x = inner_x + 2 * _WALL\n    outer_y = inner_y + 2 * _WALL\n    height = lift + _POCKET\n\n    body = Box(outer_x, outer_y, height, align=(Align.CENTER, Align.CENTER, Align.MIN))\n    # Overshoot the top so the boolean cannot leave a film roofing the pocket.\n    cutter = Box(\n        inner_x,\n        inner_y,\n        _POCKET + 1.0,\n        align=(Align.CENTER, Align.CENTER, Align.MIN),\n    ).moved(Location((0, 0, lift)))\n    cup = body - cutter\n\n    if draft:\n        return cup\n\n    # Mating pocket and bed stay sharp. Polish only the outer rim and the four\n    # vertical corners, which end on the bed rather than lying in it.\n    bed = cup.bounding_box().min.Z\n    top = cup.bounding_box().max.Z\n    half_x = outer_x / 2\n    half_y = outer_y / 2\n    inside = set(concave_edges(cup))\n\n    def outer_exposed(edge):\n        if edge in inside:\n            return False\n        c = edge.center()\n        on_outer = abs(abs(c.X) - half_x) < 1e-4 or abs(abs(c.Y) - half_y) < 1e-4\n        if not on_outer:\n            return False\n        bb = edge.bounding_box()\n        lies_on_bed = bb.max.Z <= bed + 1e-4\n        return not lies_on_bed\n\n    keep = cup.edges().filter_by(outer_exposed)\n    return polish(cup, keep, 1.0)\n"},"content":[],"locations":[]}

The foot drops into the\npocket; the solid floor under it is the lift that levels the bench.\n\n## What it is\n\nA rectangular foot cup: 2 mm walls around a through-open pocket 8 mm deep, and\na solid floor `lift` mm thick. Inner pocket is the measured leg plus 0.4 mm\nclearance so it slips on. Prints as it sits, pocket up, no supports.\n\n## Design notes\n\n- `leg_width`, `leg_depth`, and `lift` all come from `measurements.toml`. Lift\n  is still a guess; changing that file rebuilds the floor thickness.\n- Pocket is an open rectangular prism. Nothing chamfers or fillets into it.\n- Outer rim and vertical corners take the 1 mm polish pass. The bed face and\n  the mating pocket stay sharp.\n- Clearance 0.4 mm, wall 2 mm, and pocket 8 mm are specified, not measured.\n\n## Don't\n\n- Don't chamfer or fillet the pocket. The inner size is a fit, and a lead-in\n  at the mouth is the mating-geometry veto.\n- Don't put holes in the floor. The floor is the lift.\n- Don't add skirts, gussets, or extra bulk. Volume is the walls plus the floor.\n\n## Changelog\n\n- First model: slip-over cup driven by measured leg section and provisional lift.\n"},"content":[],"locations":[]}

A solid floor under\n    the foot lifts the bench by `lift` from measurements.toml, which is still a\n    guess until someone measures the wobble.\n\n    Pocket inner size is the measured leg plus 0.4 mm clearance, 8 mm deep, with\n    2 mm walls on all four sides. {"type":"tool_call_update","toolCallId":"call-58f42004-c448-4777-a732-937113883842-38","status":"completed","content":[{"type":"diff","path":"/private/var/folders/lc/0cfr61jd4s581fv23shx6jbc0000gn/T/nurb-eval-trial-k4pndihf/project/parts/leg_cup.md","oldText":"","newText":"# leg_cup\n\nSlip-over cup for the short leg of a wobbly workbench. The foot drops into the\npocket; the solid floor under it is the lift that levels the bench.\n\n## What it is\n\nA rectangular foot cup: 2 mm walls around a through-open pocket 8 mm deep, and\na solid floor `lift` mm thick. Inner pocket is the measured leg plus 0.4 mm\nclearance so it slips on. Prints as it sits, pocket up, no supports.\n\n## Design notes\n\n- `leg_width`, `leg_depth`, and `lift` all come from `measurements.toml`. Lift\n  is still a guess; changing that file rebuilds the floor thickness.\n- Pocket is an open rectangular prism. Nothing chamfers or fillets into it.\n- Outer rim and vertical corners take the 1 mm polish pass. The bed face and\n  the mating pocket stay sharp.\n- Clearance 0.4 mm, wall 2 mm, and pocket 8 mm are specified, not measured.\n\n## Don't\n\n- Don't chamfer or fillet the pocket. The inner size is a fit, and a lead-in\n  at the mouth is the mating-geometry veto.\n- Don't put holes in the floor. The floor is the lift.\n- Don't add skirts, gussets, or extra bulk. Volume is the walls plus the floor.\n\n## Changelog\n\n- First model: slip-over cup driven by measured leg section and provisional lift.\n","_meta":{"details":[{"old_string":"","old_line":1,"new_string":"# leg_cup\n\nSlip-over cup for the short leg of a wobbly workbench.
